Beyond the animals come six pairs of human figures: two pairs each of military officials, dressed in armour and carrying cudgels and swords; two of civil officials; and two of meritorious officials holding tablets of audience, immaculately dressed and standing in solemn silence. Beyond the stone figures stands the memorial archway known as the Lattice Gate, also known as the Dragon and Phoenix Gate. Its three openings are formed by columns resembling ornamental pillars. In the centre of the epistyles above each of the openings is a stone carving of a flaming pearl, which gives the archway yet another name, "the flaming archway".

Passing through the Lattice Gate, the Sacred Way leads over two bridges, the first with five arches and the second with seven, and proceeds directly to the Changling, while a branch heads off to the west and joins the Sacred Ways to the Zhaoling and Dingling.

The Excavation of the Dingling

The Dingling contains the remains of the Ming Emperor or Wanli and his two empresses, Xiaoduan and Xiaojing.

Zhu Yijun (1573-1620), the Wanli Emperor, was the thirteen emperor of the Ming Dynasty. He came to power at the age of ten, and occupied the throne for 58 years. His posthumous title is: "The great emperor conforming to the paradigm; holding to divine principles; kindhearted scholar and soldier; displaying filial obedience."

Madame Wang, the first wife of Emperor Wanli, was granted the title of Empress Xiaoduan in 1578. She died in 1620, the same year as Wanli himself. Her posthumous title is: "The great empress showing filial obedience; dignified and intelligent; faithful and kindhearted; mother of the crown prince."

Empress Xiaojing was an imperial maid serving in the Palace of Kindness and Quietude. She was raised to the rank of imperial concubine when she became pregnant with the first son of the Wanli Emperor, Zhu Zhangluo, in 1582. In 1606 she became Honourable Imperial Concubine, and in 1611 died of illness, but because of her status could not be

buried in an imperial tomb. When Emperor Wanli died in 1620, Zhu Zhangluo succeeded him on the throne. He brought up the issue of his mother's posthumous title in court on one occasion, but died before the matter could be resolved. When his son, Zhu Youxiao ascended the throne, he granted Xiaojing the title: "The holy empress; respectful and modest; kindhearted and docile; mother of the crown prince."

Though Emperor Wanli and Xiaoduan died at different time, they were buried together in the Dingling in 1620. Since by this time Xiaojing had been granted the posthumous title of "empress", her remains were transferred to the Dingling at this time and buried together with those of the emperor.

Located at the foot of Dayu Mountain in the northwest section of the tomb complex, the site for the Dingling was selected by Emperor Wanli himself and the tomb completed by imperial order before the time of his death. Construction went on from 1585 to 1590 and cost eight million taels of silver, a sum equal to all the taxes collected in China during a two-year period in the Wanli reign.

The Dingling is a faithful reproduction of the Yongling, the tomb of Emperor Jiajing (reigned 1522-1567). In front of the tomb there is a stone stele mounted on a carved stone tortoise with the head of a hornless dragon. This stele, known as the Blank Stele, has no inscription carved on its surface.

All that remains of the first entrance gate is the foundation. The second gate, now serving as the main entrance of the Dingling, is pierced with three arched passageways and topped by a gate tower with a single—eaved roof covered with yellow glazed tiles. This gate is connected to the wall which surrounds the Dingling.

Inside this wall stands another gate called The Gate of Heavenly Favour, which was destroyed at the beginning of the Qing Dynasty, rebuilt during the Qianlong reign, and damaged once again in 1914. The extant structure is linked with a wall that encircles a courtyard. Beyond the Gate of Heavenly Favour is the Hall of Heavenly Favour, where

major offerings were made to the emperor's memory. Like the gate before it, the hall was damaged at the beginning of the Qing Dynasty and rebuilt during the Qianlong period. In 1914 it was destroyed once again along with the gate, so all that is left today is its raised platform foundation surrounded by a carved stone balustrade.

To the rear of the Hall of Heavenly Favour is a Lattice Gate in the form of a memorial archway. Square pillars on each side of the gate are topped with carved stone animals, while its lintels are covered with yellow glazed tiles.

In front of the huge Memorial shrine (minglou) there is a stone offering table holding an incense burner and pairs of candlesticks and vases. The Memorial shrine is the tallest building in the Dingling complex and serves as a symbol for the entire tomb. Built entirely of stone and bricks, each of its four walls is pierced with an arched opening which leads to the stele chamber in the tower's interior. The shrine is designed in the manner of wooden palace architecture, with bracket systems supporting a multi-eaved roof covered with yellow glazed tubular tiles. A horizontal panel hanging below the eaves is inscribed the two characters "Dingling" in gold. The tall stele inside rests on a square pedestal engraved with dragon and cloud patterns. The two characters (Great Ming) are carved on the stele's crown and its body bears the inscription: "Tomb of the Great Emperor Shenzong", the temple name of the Wanli Emperor.

The excavation of the Dingling began in May 1956 and was carried out in preparation for the excavation of the larger Changling. The Dingling was chosen because of the relatively late date of its construction, because of the extended period of time it took to complete, and due to the fact that access to the underground burial chambers was facilitated by the discovery of a number of visual clues about the location of the entrance.

The underground palace lies beneath the walled mound to the rear of the memorial shrine Entrance was gained through a small opening on the surface of the mound where a stone slab, carved with the revealing inscription "The door of the tunnel" was discovered. Beyond this lay the brick-lined passageway leading down to the underground

palace. At the end of this passageway there was a stele inscribed: "It is 16 zhang (52.8m) ahead and 3.5 zhang (11.6m) down from this stele to the wall of the guardian warriors." This discovery paved the path to the eventual opening of the underground palace.

The excavation proceeded along the inclined passageway which wound its way down in an ever—narrowing spiral and ended at the "wall of the guardian warriors", a brick wall with a triangular opening in it. The wall had been built after the sarcophagi had been placed in the tomb chamber. The opening in the wall was caused by pressure from the accumulation of soil in the passageway over the centuries. Directly beyond this wall lay the arched antechamber which served as the entranceway to the underground palace.

The antechamber is a square 7.9 metres on a side, with its walls rising in an arch to the ceiling 7.3 metres high. The western wall forms the outer wall of the underground palace, and on the other side of it lies the outer chamber. Here there are two tall doors fabricated of individual slabs of white marble. They are furnished with a total of 81 hemispherical knobs laid out in nine horizontal and vertical rows and two finely carved door handles.

A legend tells that the insides of the doors were fitted with secret devices capable of killing intruders, yet the only object discovered by the archaeologists who opened the tomb was a rectangular slab of marble used to seal the tomb from the interior. To open the doors, a stiff wire loop was inserted through a crack and placed over the top of the slab. By pushing gently on the wire, the slab was gradually lowered to the ground and the doors opened for the first time in more than three centuries.

The outer chamber of the underground palace is rectangular in shape, 20 metres long, 6 metres wide and 7.2 metres high. The floor is paved with square bricks on which pine boards had been laid. Wheel tracks were found on these boards, suggesting that their purpose was to prevent the smooth surface of the bricks from being defaced by the wheeled vehicle (called a "dragon hearse") used to transport the sarcophagi and coffins into the burial

chamber.

The central chamber, a continuation of the outer chamber, is 32 metres long, 6 metres wide and 7.2 metres tall. Arched openings lead from the centres of the side walls through vestibules to the left and right side chambers. The central chamber contains three stone spirit thrones for the emperor and his empresses, as well as stone bases for the five ritual vessels and "eternal lamps".

Beyond the central chamber lies the rear, or burial chamber, where the coffins of the emperor and his empresses were discovered. This is the most important room in the underground palace, and is larger than both the outer and central chambers: 30.1 metres long, 9.1 metres wide and 9.5 metres from floor to ceiling. The floor is paved with finely polished multi-colored stones. At the centre of this chamber is a platform built to hold the three sarcophagi. Emperor Wanli's sarcophagus stood in the middle, with those Empress Xiaoduan and Xiaojing beside it. In front of the platform the floor is also covered with wooden boards.

The left and right side chambers contain large coffin platforms but there were no coffins found on them when the tomb was opened. These chambers are 26 metres long, 7.1 metres wide and 7.4 meters from floor to ceiling. Exit corridors lead out of these chambers from their western walls.

The emperor and empresses' sarcophagi and coffins are similar in design and construction, and nearly identical in size. The inner coffins are made of $\text{nanmu}$ wood lacquered inside and out side; the sarcophagi are made of lacquered pine. The sarcophagi of Wanli and Xiaoduan were discovered in poor condition, while their coffins were found relatively intact. Both the sarcophagus and coffin of Xiaojing were badly deteriorated, making restoration nearly impossible. Inside the coffin, Wanli's remains were covered with a quilt of brocade, and under that a neatly folded full-length robe and several pieces of brocade fabric. The corpse rested on another brocade quilt, under which there were a total of nine layers of blankets, quilts and felt pads. One of the blankets had 17 gold coins marked "propitiousness and good fortune" sewed to it. Wanli was dressed in an embryo

dered burial gown and was wearing a jade belt, black gauze cap, yellow damask trousers and tall boots of plain red satin. Gold and jade burial objects were placed in the coffin above the head and below the feet, while various fabrics and gold and silver objects were placed to his sides. The bottom of the coffin was lined with rolls of silk and folded robes.

In Xiaoduan's coffin, the uppermost layer was a satin quilt, beneath which were folded robes and objects of gold and lacquerware. Her corpse rested on a brocade quilt supported by four additional quilts, one of which was sewn with 100 gold coins marked "longevity and absence of calamities". The empress was wearing a pointed black gauze cap decorated with gold hairpins, and was dressed in a yellow lined jacket embroidered with dragons, a pair of yellow satin lined trousers with embroidered lotuses, a long skirt with dragons on it, and yellow satin shoes.

Xiaojing's burial accouterments are generally similar to those of Xiaoduan, however she was buried with two sets of jewellery, one worn on her head from her first burial, and the other placed beside her head when her remains were transferred to the Dingling. Under the remains are 11 blankets, and beneath them a pile of paper money and strings of copper cash with the mark of the Wanli reign period. To both sides of the corpse were silver jars, plates, bowls, spittoons and soap dishes.

In addition to the objects mentioned above which were placed inside the coffin, blue and white porcelain, tri-colour porcelain and objects of jade were placed in the empty space between the sarcophagi and coffins, while wooden ceremonial funerary objects were placed on top of the sarcophagi. A total of 29 chests containing burial objects were placed in different parts of the burial chamber. Their contents include: gold and silver objects, crowns, belts, pendants, bronze and tin funerary objects, weapons, scrolls recording posthumous titles, posthumous seals and wooden figures.

The excavation of the Dingling lasted a total of 26 months. In October 1959 the Dingling Museum was opened to the public.

Silk Fabrics

More than 600 specimens of silk fabrics were discovered in the Dingling, including material for imperial robes, cloth in bolts and decorative fabrics. Most of the silk fabrics are of the Jacquard weave; the remainder include several varieties of damask silk, plain silk and gauze. A total of 177 bolts of fabric were found, all neatly rolled around cores of straw, folded and tied with three loops of silk thread. Most of the rolls had paper labels attached, recording the colour, pattern, material, use and length, while some included the specific name of the material, place of origin, date of weaving, name of weaver, head of the mill and supervisor of production.

Of the many varieties of silk unearthed in bolts, the colourful zanghua is the most typical. The term zanghua first appeared in a Ming Dynasty text. The material was most likely woven in the traditional brocade method, incorporating techniques used in making silk tapestry. Although this technique had been employed prior to the Ming Dynasty, its application to silk manufacture was a great achievement of the Ming.

The principal design motifs of zanghua silk are dragons and phoenixes, mythological creatures which became symbols of prestige for emperors and their empresses. Dragons are depicted in a wide variety of active postures, frequently set off against a background of clouds and ocean waves. Phoenixes are often combined with flower and plant patterns to highlight their gracefulness. Designs of other animals, fish, insects and plants were chosen for their symbolic meaning of because their names were homophones for auspicious words, such as "wealth", "longevity" or "happiness". For example, the design entitled jiqing youyu (good fortune and felicitations in abundance) is composed of a halberd $\langle ji\rangle$ , chimes $(qing)$ and two fish $(yu)$ . The design entitled fushou (happiness and longevity) consists of a bat (bianfu) and the Chinese character for longevity (shou). The designs executed on silk combined the modest simplicity characteristic of Ming taste with the sumptuous and elaborate style of the Tang. The colours used in zanghua silk are

lively and varied. The fabrics of the Tang and Song Dynasties are characterized by harmonious colour combinations, while those of the Yuan introduce the use of gold thread. Ming fabrics combine both of these characteristics and make use of peacock feathers as well. Yellow, blue, red and green are the principal colours.

The fabrics used for making imperial robes display a particularly high level of weaving technique. Among the 400 garments and fragments found in the Dingling are gunfu (emperor's ceremonial gowns), shang (a skirt worn in ancient China) and bixi (a kind of apron), all of which the emperor would wear during grand ceremonies; dragon robes for appearances at court; and jiangsha (deep red silk gauze) robes and daoist robes with wide sleeves worn for everyday use. There is also a wide variety of women's dress, such as lined, unlined and cotton—padded jackets, skirts, trousers, shoes and socks.

The emperor would wear gunfu for such ceremonies as worshipping heaven or his ancestors, paying respects to the gods of the earth, grain, agriculture, etc., as well as celebrating important days in the traditional ritual calendar. Five gunfu were discovered in the tomb, three of which are embroidered and two of brocade. In addition to twelve dragons, each gunfu has designs of the sun, moon, strars, mountains and pheasants on its back and sleeves. There are also designs of ritual vessels, aquatic plants, flames, rice grains and weapons. The designs illustrate the emperor's virtues: his expertise in literature as well as the martial arts; his ability to handle the affairs of state in a wise and decisive manner; the way he illuminates every corner of the empire like the sun, and bestows his bounty upon the people, etc.

Dragon robes were the court and daily dress of the emperor. A total of 61 were found in the tomb. The majority are made of woven silk with the remainder of satin and gauze. The robes can be divided into two types according to the style of collar.

A number of zhondan, or "lining gowns" worn between the outer robe and underwear were unearthed. There are three kinds: lined, unlined and gowns padded with silk floss.

They are made of either satin, woven silk or damask silk, but none are made of gauze, suggesting that the materials used in their manufacture were not strictly chosen. Like dragon robes, zhondan were made with two different styles of collar.

Shang skirts and bixi aprons were worn during ceremonial occasions. Shang were made of plain yellow silk gauze embroidered with flames, ritual vessels, aquatic plants, rice and weapons. Bixi aprons, made of red silk gauze with a gauze lining, are wide on the top and narrow on the bottom. They are decorated with embroidered designs of dragons and flames in the shape of peaches.

More than 100 women's gowns were found in the coffin of the two empresses. The materials used include satin, woven silk, zanghua, gauze and velvet. They are all designed in similar style, with wide sleeves and standing, square or round collars. Most of them are decorated with square patches of embroidery or brocade, two on the front and two on the back. The principle decorative patterns are embroidered dragons sporting in the clouds, but there are also dragons paired with phoenixes, flowers and Chinese characters with auspicious meanings. The clasps on the women's garments are of two types: silk loops, and gold or gilded bronze buttons. They appear both separately and together on the same garment.

A total of 47 skirts made of such materials as satin, gauze and other kinds of silk were found in the tombs. Most of them are of the pleated variety, made in two parts, each consisting of three and one half widths of fabric joined together at the waist. Red, yellow and green are the principle colours used.

The pair of high—heeled shoes with pointed toes found in the coffin of Empress Xiaojing is the only extant pair of high—heeled shoes dating from the Ming Dynasty. The insteps are made of darkly figured satin, the vamps are embroidered with lotuses, while the toes have designs of pines and bamboos. The heels are constructed of several layers of thick straw paper sewn together with silk thread and wrapped in plain red satin.

The unlined tall boots and felt boots discovered in the coffin of Emperor Wanli are also unique finds. The section covering the knees is made of silk tapestry, and the part covering the legs of plain red satin. There is an insole of thin leather, and the body of the boots is made of a double layer of coarse white cotton lined with palm fibres stitched together with silk thread and coated with a white powder.

Among the embroidered items in the Dingling perhaps the finest are the two robes with the "hundred boys" patterns found in the tomb of Empress Xiaojing. The robes are made of silk gauze with standing collars. The design consists of nine dragong embroidered in gold thread, the Eight Treasures, mountains, trees, flowers and plants, ingeniously combined with the hundred boys to form many vivid tableaux.

Silk tapestry is a weaving technique developed in ancient China. Previous to the Tang Dynasty it was used in garments worn by emperors, empresses and nobles. During the Tang and Song Dynasties the technique was used to make exquisite reproductions of calligraphy and painting and thus came to be appreciated as a fine art. However it continued to be put to practical use as can be demonstrated by the silk tapestry fabrics found in the Dingling.

Gold and Silver Objects

Some 500 gold and silver treasures, many of them objects of daily use in the imperial palace were found in the tombs. The gold and silver objects were manufactured in moulds, with the decorative elements executed in relief and filigree, or with inlaid patterns composed of pearls and precious stones. Many of the pieces bear inscriptions giving the date of manufacture, name of the object, material used, weight and the names of the artisan and supervisory official. Notable among these finds are the wine vessels, in particular a gold wine pitcher and goblet with a gold tray in-laid with pearls and precious stones. The gold used in its manufacture is approximately 22.5 carat.

Another remarkable object is the peach-shaped censer inlaid with pearls and precious stones. Censers of gold and silver were first made in the Han Dynasty. A contemporary

text describing lift in the imperial capital mentions a revolving incense burner flat in shape that could be used safely in bed. In 1970, an object fitting this description was unearthed from a Tang Dynasty hoard near Xi'an, and numerous other examples have been discovered in Ming tombs.

The Dingling tomb also contained more than 100 ingots of tribute gold. The largest examples, weighing ten taels, have an actual weight of 370 to 380 grams. Each ingot's purity, weight, source, date of offering, as well as the name of the refiner and artisan were either inscribed on the surface of the ingot or written on a label attached to it.

Porcelain

The eight prunus vases (meiping) and the huge jar with dragon designs found in the underground palace are fine examples of Ming blue and white porcelain. The "dragon vase" which stood in the central chamber of the tomb is also known as the "eternal lamp". A bronze wick holder was set afloat on the surface of the oil that filled the jar. The inscription dates the jar from the Jiajing reign period. Other objects of interest include the tri—colour incense burner and beaker—shaped vase.

Jade and Jewellery

The Dingling jade finds include ritual objects and articles of daily use, such as gobiets, ewers, basins, spittoons and cups. More than 240 pieces of jewellery were unearthed from the emperor and empresses' coffins. Undoubtedly the most valuable among them are the hairpins made of gold, silver, copper, jade, amber, tortoise shell and other precious stones. Notable is Xiaoduan's hairpin inlaid with gems and a jade dragon playing with a pearl, which was found stuck in her pointed hat.

Hats, Belts and Pendants

The headgear unearthed from the Dingling were worn by the emperor and his empresses on both formal and informal occasions. The former include erowns (guan) worn by the emperor, and phoenix crowns worn by the empresses during grand ceremonies; in addition, there are leather caps

known as bian worn by the emperor while carrying out imperial inspections, proclaiming edicts, receiving tribute or making pilgrimages.

The most splendid examples of imperial headgear are the dragon and phoenix crowns worn by the empresses. A total of four such crowns were discovered in the Dingling, two belonging to each of the empresses. Jade, gold and other precious stones were used in the manufacture of these crowns. Their weights range from 2.1 to 2.9 kilograms, and the most elaborate of them has 5,449 pearls worked into the design.

Leather belts, which symbolize the wearer's position, were usually worn by the emperor and empress outside of their robes. The belts unearthed from the Dingling are made of a single strip of leather sandwiched between layers of red or yellow satin. The belts are divided into three sections which are attached to an inner belt made of two layers of yellow satin. The length of the belt can be adjusted by means of a belt hook. The belts in the Dingling are decorated with ornaments made of gold, jade, rhinoceros horn and silver. Jade belts were worn only by the emperor, empress, imperial concubines, crown princes and a few high officials. Wanli's coffin contained 14 filigree gold belts inlaid with precious stones. Belt hooks found in the tombs were made of white jade, jasper, onyx and wood.

In ancient times, the emperor and his subjects would wear sets of jade pendants on their belts which produced pleasant tinkling sounds with every bodily movement. In early times, pendants were worn only by literati and officials, but in later dynasties the rules governing specific articles of dress became more complex. In the Ming Dynasty, pendants were worn by the emperor and empress while attending formal ceremonies. The dynastic history of the Ming recorded the precise number of jade pieces that made up a set of pendants, and even specified the shape of each piece. The pendants found in the Dingling are similar in shape and number to the descriptions in the text. White jade is the most commonly used material, and many of the pieces are carved with patterns of dragons and clouds.

A unique object of dress unearthed in the Dingling is

the short embroidered cape worn by the empress. The satin-lined cape is divided into two parts. Cloud and dragon patterns are executed in gold thread and there are additional decorations composed of pearls, gold leaf, filigree and jade.

The tomb also contained several funerary objects, including weapons cast of bronze and tin, most of which can be identified by the inscriptions on the labels attached to them.
